We estimate the correlations between all pairs of residuals from the second-stage principal component analysis from specific sectors. The correlations that are higher than 0.15 in absolute value are shown as black dots.

Figure 4:
Partial correlations of second-stage residuals.

We estimate the partial correlations between all pairs of residuals from the second-stage LASSO regression from specific sectors. The correlations that are higher than 0.15 in absolute value are shown as black dots.

To shed some light on the links among different sectors, we report how often variables from sector
i
are selected in the third-stage LASSO regression for firms in sector
j
. The numbers are normalized by the total number of firms in each sector and are presented in Figure
5
. The most interesting fact is that covariates from the financial sector are the ones most frequently selected for all the other sectors. Other sectors, such as Mining, Chemical, Machinery, Electrical Equipment, Manufacturing, and Retail are also frequently selected. This may indicate that there are industry factors, specifically a “financial factor”, that is unmodeled in the first two stages. However, if we augment the set of regressors in the first stage by the value-weighted portfolio from the financial sector, although the remaining dependence among firms is attenuated, particularly for Department Stores, we do not get close to an exact factor model. This finding suggests that there are hidden links among firms.

Figure 5:
Variable Selection Frequency.

We report how often the variables from column sectors are selected in the third-stage LASSO regression for firms on row sectors. The numbers are normalized by the total number of firms in each sector.

4.2
Forecasting US Industrial Production

The second application consists of forecasting monthly US industrial production using a large set of monthly macroeconomic variables. We compare four different models: (1) Autoregressive model; (2) Sparse LASSO Regression (SR); (3) Principal Component Regression (PCR); and (4)
FarmPredict
.

We use variables from the August 2022 vintage of the FRED-MD database, which is a large monthly macroeconomic dataset designed for empirical analysis in data-rich macroeconomic environments. The dataset is updated in real-time through the FRED database and is available from Michael McCraken’s webpage.
7
7

7

https://research.stlouisfed.org/econ/mccracken/fred-databases/. For further details, we refer to
McCracken and Ng 2016
.
.

Our sample extends from January 1960 to December 2019 (719 observations), and only variables with all observations in the period are used (122 variables). The dataset is divided into eight groups: (i) output and income; (ii) labor market; (iii) housing; (iv) consumption, orders, and inventories; (v) money and credit; (vi) interest and exchange rates; (vii) prices; and (viii) stock market. Finally, all series are transformed in order to become stationary.

In order to highlight the gains of exploring all relevant information in the dataset, we construct one-step ahead forecasts for the first-order difference of the logarithm of the monthly industrial production index (IP, growth rate):
Y_{IP,t}
.

We compare the following models:

1.

Autoregressive model (
AR
):

\widehat{Y}_{IP,t+1|t}^{(\texttt{AR})}=\widehat{\phi}_{0}+\widehat{\phi}_{1}\widehat{Y}_{IP,t}+\ldots+\widehat{\phi}_{p}\widehat{Y}_{IP,t-p+1},

where
\widehat{\phi}_{0},\widehat{\phi}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\phi}_{p}
are OLS estimates. The value of
p
is selected by BIC.

2.

Sparse regression (
SR
):

\widehat{Y}_{IP,t+1|t}^{(\texttt{SR})}=\widehat{\beta}_{0}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}+\ldots+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{p}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{Y}_{t-p+1},

\widehat{\beta}_{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{p}
are LASSO estimates and
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}=(Y_{1t},\ldots,Y_{nt})
with
n=122
. The penalty parameter is selected by modified BIC as in
Wang, Li and Leng 2009
.

3.

Principal Component Regression (
PCR
):

\widehat{Y}_{IP,t+1|t}^{(\texttt{PCR})}=\widehat{\pi}_{0}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}+\cdots+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\pi}}_{q}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t-q+1},

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}
is the estimate of the
(k\times 1)
vector of factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{t}
given by the first
k
principal components of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
being the sample average of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}
. The parameters of the model are computed by OLS regression of
Y_{j,t}
on a constant and lags of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}
. The lag
q
is selected by BIC.

4.

AR - Principal Component Regression (
AR-PCR
):

\widehat{Y}_{IP,t+1|t}^{(\texttt{AR-PCR})}=\widehat{\alpha}_{0}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}^{\prime}Y_{IP,t}+\ldots+\widehat{\alpha}_{p}Y_{IP,t-p+1}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}+\cdots+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\varrho}}_{p}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t-p+1},

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}
is the estimate of the
(k\times 1)
vector of factors
\boldsymbol{F}_{t}
given by the first
k
principal components of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
being the sample average of
\boldsymbol{Y}_{t}
. The parameters of the model are computed by OLS regression of
Y_{j,t}
on a constant, its own lags and lags of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}
. The lag orders
p
and
q
are selected by BIC.

5.

FarmPredict
:

\widehat{Y}_{i,t+1|t}^{(\texttt{FarmPredict})}=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{j}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}_{j1}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}+\cdots+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}_{jp}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t-p+1}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{t}+\ldots+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{pi}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{t-p+1},

where

\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{t}=\left(\widehat{U}_{1,t},\ldots,\widehat{U}_{n,t}\right)^{\prime}
and
\widehat{U}_{i,t}=Y_{i,t}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}
,
i\in[n]
. The estimates
\widehat{\theta}_{0i},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{1i}\ldots,\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{pi}
,
i\in[n]
, are given by LASSO. The penalty parameter is selected by the modified BIC and the value of
p
is set to 24.

The forecasts are based on a rolling-window framework of a fixed length of 480 observations, starting in January 1960. Therefore, the forecasts start on January 1990. The last forecasts are for December 2019. Note that the
AR
model only considers information concerning the own past of the variable of interest.
SR
and
PCR
/
AR-PCR
/ expand the information by two opposing routes. While
SR
uses a sparse combination of the set of variables,
PCR
and
AR-PCR
consider a factor structure (dense model). In the case of
AR-PCR
, lags of the dependent variable are also included.
FarmPredict
combines these two approaches and uses the full information available. The number of factors is set to 1.

Figure
6
reports the ratios of cumulative MSE of the
FarmPredict
model against the cumulative MSE of the other benchmarks over the forecasting period. Several conclusions emerge from the plot. First,
FarmPredict
outperforms the PCR model over the entire out-of-sample period. It is also, in general, superior to the AR, SR, and AR-PCR models, apart from 2004 and 2008. During this period, the economy experienced housing and financial crises. Furthermore, the number of out-of-sample forecasting periods was also quite small. It is clear that the performance of
FarmPredict
improves drastically after 2008, and over the entire sample, the MSE ratio of the
FarmPredict
model over the AR benchmark is 0.9080, while the SR, PCR, and AR-PCR models have the following ratios, respectively: 0.9217, 1.0249, and 0.9215.

Figure 6:
Ratios of cumulative MSEs.

In the figure we report the cumulative ratios of the mean squared errors (MSE) of the
FarmPredict
model and the other benchmarks over the rolling windows.

5
Conclusions

We propose a new methodology that bridges the gap between sparse regressions and factor models and evaluates the gains of increasing the information set via factor augmentation. Our proposal consists of several steps. In the first one, we filter the data for known factors (trends, seasonal adjustments, covariates). In the second step, we estimate a latent factor structure. Finally, in the last part of the procedure, we estimate a sparse regression for the idiosyncratic components. We also propose a new test for remaining structures in both high-dimensional covariance and partial covariance matrices. Our test can be used to evaluate the benefits of adding more structure to the model. Our paper has also a number of important side results. First, we proved the consistency of kernel estimation of long-run covariance matrices in high dimensions where both the number of observations and variables grows. Second, we derive the theoretical properties of factor estimation on the residuals of a first-step process. Third, the proposed test can be used as a diagnostic tool for factor models.

We evaluate our methodology with simulations and real data. The simulations show the test has good size and power properties even when the true number of factors is unknown and must be determined from the data. If the number of factors is underestimated, we observe size distortions. In particular, this is the case when the eigenvalue ratio test is used to determine the number of latent factors. The simulations also show that there are major informational gains when combining factor models and sparse regressions in a forecasting exercise. Two applications are considered.

Supplementary Material

CONTENTS

S.1
Introduction

The goal of this supplement is to provide additional results as well as the proofs of all theoretical results in the main body of the paper.

This Supplementary Material is organized as follows. We start by discussing guidelines for practical implementation of the method in Section
S.2
. Section
S.3
provides some additional simulation results. Section
S.4
presents results for the case with geometric mixing and Exponential Tails. Section
S.5
contains all the proofs of the results on paper. Finally, Section
S.7
collects auxiliary lemmas.

S.2
Guide to Practice

The methodology in this paper involves several steps. The first step consists of identifying known covariates that we may want to control for. It may involve the removal of deterministic trends and seasonal effects, for instance. This can be done either by parametric or nonparametric regressions. It is important to notice, however, that the convergence rates of the estimations in the subsequent steps will be influenced by the convergence rate of the estimation in the first part of the procedure.

After the data are filtered in the first step, one can test for the remaining covariance structure. If the covariance matrix of the filtered data is (almost) diagonal, there is no need to estimate a latent factor structure, and the practitioner may jump directly to the third step.
On the other hand, if the covariance of the filtered data is dense, a latent factor model should be considered, and the number of factors must be determined. To determine the number of factors, we consider either the eigenvalue ratio test of
Horenstein 2013
or the information criteria put forward in
Bai and Ng 2002
. The factors can be estimated by the usual methods.

The next step involves a sparse regression in order to estimate any remaining links between idiosyncratic components. Before running the last step, we may test for a diagonal covariance matrix of the idiosyncratic terms. If the null is not rejected, there is no need for additional estimation. In case of rejection, we can proceed with a LASSO regression. We recommend that the penalty term is selected by some Information Criterion (IC) as advocated by
Wang, Li and Leng 2009
and
Medeiros and Mendes 2016
. If out-of-sample forecasting is the goal, an additional final step is necessary. In this case, lags of factors and idiosyncratic terms must be determined. The practitioner may rely on the usual information criteria available.

Finally, concerning the estimation of the long-run matrices, the usual methods discussed in the literature can be used here to select the kernel and the bandwidth. We use the simple Bartlett kernel with bandwidth given as
\lfloor T/3\rfloor
.

S.3
Simulation

In this section, we report simulation results divided into two parts. In the first one, we evaluate the finite-sample properties of the test for the remaining covariance structure. In the second part, we highlight the informational gains when considering both the common factors and the idiosyncratic component. We simulate 1,000 replications of the following model for various combinations of sample size (
T
) and number of variables (
n
):

\displaystyle Y_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}+U_{i,t},\,i=[n],\,t=[T],

(S.1)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}_{t}

\displaystyle=0.8\boldsymbol{F}_{t-1}+\boldsymbol{E}_{t},

(S.2)

\displaystyle U_{i,t}

\displaystyle=I(i=1)(\theta_{12}U_{2t}+\theta_{13}U_{3t}+\theta_{14}U_{4t}+\theta_{15}U_{5t})+V_{i,t}

(S.3)

\displaystyle V_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\phi V_{it-1}+\epsilon_{i,t}

(S.4)

where
I(\cdot)
is the indicator function,
\{\epsilon_{i,t}\}
is a sequence of independent t-distributed random variables with 10 degrees of freedom, and
\{\boldsymbol{E}_{t}\}
is a sequence of
r
-dimensional mutually independent random vectors t-distributed with 10 degrees of freedom. Furthermore,
\{\epsilon_{i,t}\}
and
\{\boldsymbol{E}_{t}\}
are mutually independent for all time periods, factors and variables. For each Monte Carlo replication, the vector of loadings is sampled from a Gaussian distribution with mean -6 and standard deviation 0.2 for
i=1
and mean 2 and unit variance for
i=2,\ldots,n
. The coefficients
\theta_{12}
,
\theta_{13}
,
\theta_{14}
, and
\theta_{15}
are equal to zero or 0.8, 0.9, -0.7, and 0.5, respectively. We set the number of factors,
r
, equal to 3.
\phi
can be either 0 or 0.5. Note that equation (
S.3
) is a special case of equation (
2.2
) with
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}=\boldsymbol{U}_{-i,t}
.

S.3.1
Test for Remaining Covariance Structure

We start by reporting results for the test of no remaining structure on the covariance matrix of
\boldsymbol{U}_{t}=(U_{1t},\ldots,U_{nt})^{\prime}
. The null hypothesis considered is that all the covariances between the first variable (
i=1
) and the remaining ones are all zero. For size simulations we set
\theta_{12}=\theta_{13}=\theta_{14}=\theta_{15}=0
in the DGP. To evaluate the effects of factor estimation as well as the methods in selecting the number of factors, we consider the following scenarios: (1) factors are known, and there is no estimation involved; (2) factors are estimated by principal components, but the number of factors is known; (3) the number of factors is determined by the eigenvalue ratio procedure of
Horenstein 2013
; (4)-(7) the number of factors is determined by one of the four information criteria proposed by
Bai and Ng 2002
as defined by

\begin{matrix}[l]\textnormal{IC}_{1}=\log[S(r)]+r\frac{n+T}{nT}\log\left(\frac{nT}{n+T}\right)&\textnormal{IC}_{2}=\log[S(r)]+r\frac{n+T}{nT}\log C_{nT}^{2}\\
\textnormal{IC}_{3}=\log[S(r)]+r\frac{\log C_{nT}^{2}}{C_{nT}^{2}}&\textnormal{IC}_{4}=\log[S(r)]+r\frac{(n+T-k)\log(nT)}{nT}.\end{matrix}

where
S(r)=\frac{1}{nT}\|\boldsymbol{R}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}_{r}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{r}\|_{2}^{2}
and
C_{nT}:=\sqrt{\min(n,T)}
.

Table
S.1
and
S.2
reports the results of the empirical size of test for different significance levels. We consider the case of
\phi=0
in Table
S.1
and
\phi=0.5
in
S.2
. The tables present the results when the factors are known in panel (a), the factors are unknown but the number of factors is known in panel (b), or the number of factors is estimated either by the information criterion
\textnormal{IC}_{1}
in panel (c) or the eigenvalue ratio procedure in panel (d).

A number of facts emerge from the inspection of the results in Table
S.1
. First, size distortions are small when the factors are known. In this case, the test is undersized when the pair
(n,T)
is small. When the factor is not known but the true number of factors is available, the size distortions are high only when
T=100
and
n=50
due to inaccurate estimation of factors. However, the distortions disappear when the pair
(T,n)
grows. In this case, the empirical size is similar to the situation reported in Panel (a). The finite performance of the test in the case where the number of factors is selected by information criterion
\textnormal{IC}_{1}
is almost indistinguishable from the case reported in Panel (b). However, the results with the eigenvalue ratio procedure are much worse when
T=100
and
n=50
. In this case, the procedure selects fewer factors than the true number
r=3
. For instance, the procedure selects 2 or fewer factors in 36% of the replications. Just as comparison, for
T=100
and
n=50
,
\textnormal{IC}_{1}
underdetermines the number of factors only in 3.10% of the cases. The latter also confirms that overestimation of the number of factors will not have a big adversarial effect. For all the other combinations of
T
and
n
all the data-driven methods select the correct number of factors in almost all replications.

When the idiosyncratic components are autocorrelated the size distortions are higher, as reported in Table
S.2
. This is mainly caused by the well-known difficulties in the estimation of the long-run covariance matrix.

Table
S.3
report the results of the empirical power with
\phi=0
,
\beta_{12}=0.8
,
\beta_{13}=0.9
,
\beta_{14}=-0.7
, and
\beta_{15}=-0.5
in the DGP. When the factors are known, the test always rejects the null and the empirical power is one for any significance level. On the other hand, when factors must be estimated but the number of factors is known, the power decreases, as depicted in panel (b) in the table. Nevertheless, for
T=500,700
the power is reasonably high, especially when the test is conducted at a
10\%
significance level. For
T=100
, the performance deteriorates as
n
grows. The results are similar when data-driven procedures are used to determine the number of factors, and the conclusions are mostly the same.

Table
S.4
report power results in a similar setting as above but with
\phi=0.5
. The above conclusions are mostly the same if
\phi=0
or
\phi=0.5
.

The main message of the simulation exercise is that the finite-sample performance of the proposed tests depends on the correct selection of factors. Nevertheless, for the DGP considered here, the usual data-driven methods available in the literature to determine the true number of factors seem to work reasonably well.

S.3.2
Informational Gains

The goal of this simulation is to compare, in a prediction environment, the three-stage method developed in the paper by evaluating the information gains in predicting
Y_{1t}
by three different methods. First, the predictions are computed from a LASSO regression of
Y_{1t}
on all the other
n-1
variables. This is the Sparse Regression (SR) approach. Second, we consider a principal component regression (PCR), i.e., an ordinary least squares (OLS) regression of the variable of interest on factors computed from the pool of other variables. Finally, we consider predictions constructed from the method proposed here, the
FarmPredict
methodology. Table
S.5
presents the results. The table presents the average mean squared error (MSE) over 5-fold cross-validation (CV) subsamples. As in the size and power simulations, we consider different combinations of
T
and
n
. We report results for the case where
\theta_{12}=0.8
,
\theta_{13}=0.9
,
\theta_{14}=-0.7
, and
\theta_{15}=-0.5
in the DGP.

According to the DGP, the theoretical MSE is 0.25 when all the information is used. When just a factor is used, the MSE is 2.21. From the table is clear that there are significant informational gains when we consider both factors and the cross-dependence between idiosyncratic components. Several conclusions emerge from the table. First, it is clear that when the sample size increases the MSE reduces. This is expected. Second, the PCR’s MSE and
FarmPredict
’s MSE are close to their theoretical values of 2.21 and 0.25 when the sample increases. The performance of the
FarmPredict
is quite remarkable when
T=500
or
T=700
and is always superior to Sparse Regression and PCR.

Table S.1:
Simulation Results: Size with
\phi=0

S.4
Results for the case with Geometric Mixing and Exponential Tails

This section extends the results from the main text (Theorems
1
-
6
) to the case where the random quantities admit an exponential tail and a strong mixing coefficient with exponential decay (see Assumption
4
(a)-(c) for a precise statement).

Before we begin, we need some additional pieces of notation. For any convex function
\psi:\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\psi(0)=0
and
\psi(x)\to\infty
as
x\to\infty
and (real-valued) random variable
X
, we denote its Orlicz-norm by
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|x\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}
, which is defined by
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}:=\inf\left\{C>0:\mathbb{E}\left[\psi\left(\frac{|X|}{C}\right)\right]\leq 1\right\}
. In particular, we have the
\ell^{p}
Orlicz-norm of
X
by
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p}
for
p\in[0,\infty)
by setting
\psi(x)=x^{p}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma}}
the exponential Orlicz-norm for
\gamma>0
by setting
\psi(x)=\exp(x^{\gamma})-1
for
\gamma\geq 1
and
\psi(x)
is the convex hull of
x\mapsto\exp(x^{\gamma})-1
for
\gamma\in(0,1)
(to ensure convexity). Also, when
\boldsymbol{X}
is a random vector, we define its Orlicz-norm by
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{X}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}:=\sup_{\|\boldsymbol{u}\|\leq 1}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{u}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}
.

We strengthen Assumption (
2
.c) by adding the following condition. Recall that
\{\alpha_{m}\}_{m}
denotes the strong mixing coefficients
\{\boldsymbol{Z}_{t}\}_{t}
as defined in (
3.3
).

Assumption 4

(
Moments and Dependency: Exponential Case
)
.

. There are universal constants
C,K_{1},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}>0
such for all
t,s\in[T]
,
T\geq 2
and
i\in[n]
,

1.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{Z}_{t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\leq C

2.

\alpha_{m}\leq\exp(-K_{1}m^{\gamma_{1}})
for
1\leq m<T
.

3.

\gamma<1
where
\gamma
is defined by
1/\gamma=1/\gamma_{1}+2/\gamma_{2}

4.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|n^{-1/2}\left[\boldsymbol{U}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}_{t}-\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{U}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}_{t})\right]\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\leq C

5.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|n^{-1/2}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{j,i}U_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\leq C

6.

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\|(\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}/T)^{-1}\|\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\leq C

7.

\frac{(\log n)^{(2/\gamma)-1}}{T}\leq\frac{1}{C_{1}}
where
C_{1}
is positive constant only depending on
C
,
K_{1}
,
\gamma
and
\gamma_{1}
.

Theorem S.7
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
:

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\frac{\sqrt{r}k[\log(n)\log(nTk)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\sqrt{\log(nk)}}{\sqrt{T}}.

Theorem S.8
.

Under Assumptions
1
–
4
, let
\varrho_{R}
be a non-negative sequence of
n
and
T
such that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{R}
. Then

1.

\max_{t\leq T}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{[\log T]^{1/\gamma_{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}+\varrho_{R}[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}

2.

\max_{i\leq n}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{T}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\varrho_{R}

3.

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\log T)^{1/\gamma_{2}}\sqrt{\frac{\log n}{T}}+\frac{(\log T)^{1/\gamma_{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}+\varrho_{R}[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}
,

provided that
\sqrt{\log n/T}+[\log(nT)\varrho_{R}]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\lesssim 1
.

Theorem S.9
.

Let
\varrho_{U}
be a non-negative sequence of
n
and
T
such that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{U}
and assume that Assumptions
2
and
4
hold. For every
\epsilon>0
there is a constant
0<C_{\epsilon}<\infty
such that if the penalty parameter is set
\xi\geq C_{\epsilon}\xi_{0}
, then for any minimizer
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}
of (
3.5
), with probability at least
1-\epsilon
:

\max_{i\in[n]}\left[(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}^{\prime}\right)(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\right]\leq 8\frac{\xi^{2}s_{0}}{b^{2}},

provided that
\frac{\xi_{1}s_{0}}{b}\leq K_{\epsilon}
where
s_{k}:=\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{k}
for
k\in\{0,1,2\}
,
K_{\epsilon}
is a positive constant only depending on
\epsilon
, and

\displaystyle\xi_{0}

\displaystyle:=\left(1+s_{2}\right)\sqrt{\frac{\log[n(l+1)]}{T}}+\left(1+s_{1}\right)\left[[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}\right],

\displaystyle\xi_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sqrt{\frac{\log[n(l+1)]}{T}}+\left[[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}\right].

Theorem S.10
.

Under Assumptions
1
,
2
, and
4
, let
\varrho_{\gamma},\varrho_{U},\varrho_{\theta},\varrho_{P},\varrho_{\lambda}
and
\varrho_{F}
be non-negative sequence on
n
and
T
such that, uniformly in
i\in[n]
,
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\|_{1}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{\gamma}
,
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{U}
,
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{\theta}
,
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{P}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{P}
,
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{\lambda}
, and
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|_{2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{F}
, respectively. Then, for every
t\geq 1
,

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\widehat{Y}_{i,t}-\widetilde{Y}_{i,t}\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\varrho_{\gamma}+\varrho_{\theta})(\log n)^{1/\gamma_{2}}+\varrho_{U}s_{1}+\varrho_{P}+\varrho_{\lambda}+\varrho_{F},

where
s_{1}
is defined in Theorem
3
.

Theorem S.11
.

For
\mathcal{D}\subseteq[n]^{2}
, let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}:=\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\mathcal{D}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{D}})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}
be a zero-mean Gaussian vector with the same covariance matrix of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Sigma})
.
Under Assumptions
1
–
4
, if further

1.

\{\boldsymbol{U}_{t}:t\in[T]\}
is fourth-order stationary process for each
T
;

2.

the minimum eigenvalue of
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Sigma}
is greater or equal to
\underline{c}
, for some
\underline{c}>0
,

then,

\displaystyle\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log d+\big[\log(dT)\big]^{2/\gamma}(\log d)^{2}\log T}{\sqrt{T}\underline{c}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(\log d)^{2}+(\log d)^{3/2}\log T+\log d(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log(dT)}{T^{1/4}\underline{c}^{2}},

where
d:=|\mathcal{D}|
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}:=\sqrt{T}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{\mathcal{D}}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{\mathcal{D}})
, then

\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})\lesssim\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})+\inf_{\delta>0}\left[\delta_{1}\sqrt{1\lor\log(d/\delta)}+\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}\|_{\infty}>\delta)\right].

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Sigma}
be any positive semi definite estimator of
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Sigma}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*}|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Sigma})
, then

\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\lesssim\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})+\inf_{\delta>0}\left[\delta\log d(1\lor|\log d|)+\mathbb{P}(\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Sigma}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Sigma}\|_{\max}>\delta)\right].

Theorem S.12
.

For
\mathcal{D}\subseteq[n]^{2}
, let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}:=\sqrt{T}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}_{\mathcal{D}}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{D}})
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}
be a zero-mean Gaussian vector with the same covariance matrix of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Pi})
.
Under the same assumptions and notation of Theorem
S.11
with
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Sigma}
and
\underline{c}
replaced by
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Pi}
and
\underline{b}
, respectively in condition (c), we have

\displaystyle\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log d+\big[\log(dT)\big]^{2/\gamma}(\log d)^{2}\log T}{\sqrt{T}\underline{b}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(\log d)^{2}+(\log d)^{3/2}\log T+\log d(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log(dT)}{T^{1/4}\underline{b}^{2}},

where
d:=|\mathcal{D}|
.

Let
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}:=\sqrt{T}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}_{\mathcal{D}}-\boldsymbol{\Pi}_{\mathcal{D}})
, then

\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})\lesssim\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})+\inf_{\delta>0}\left[\delta_{1}\sqrt{1\lor\log(d/\delta)}+\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}\|_{\infty}>\delta)\right].

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Pi}
be any positive semi definite estimator of
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Pi}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}^{*}|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Pi})
, then

\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}^{*})\lesssim\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})+\inf_{\delta>0}\left[\delta\log d(1\lor|\log d|)+\mathbb{P}(\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}_{\Pi}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}_{\Pi}\|_{\max}>\delta)\right].

S.5
Proof of the Theorems

S.5.1
Proof of Theorems
1
and
S.7

We first upper bound
|\widehat{R}_{i,t}-R_{i,t}|
. Recall that
\widehat{R}_{i,t}-R_{i,t}=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}
. Then, by subsequent application of Hölder’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{R}_{i,t}-R_{i,t}|\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\|\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\|\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\|\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|\leq k\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\|\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\|_{\infty}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty},

(S.1)

where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}:=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i}/T
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}:=\boldsymbol{X}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{R}_{i}/T
. Therefore,

\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}\leq k\left(\max_{i}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\right\|\right)\left(\max_{i,j}\left|\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{i}\right|\right)\left(\max_{i,t,j}\left|X^{(j)}_{i,t}\right|\right),

(S.2)

where
\widehat{v}_{i}^{(j)}
and
X_{i,t}^{(j)}
denote the
j
-th component of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
and
\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}
respectively.

Under Assumption
2
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\|\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}\leq C
. By definition,
R_{i,t}=\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}+U_{i,t}
, then

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|R_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}\leq\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}\leq(1+\sqrt{r}\|\Lambda\|_{\max}){\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{Z}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}\leq(1+C\sqrt{r})C

under Assumptions
3
and
2
. Also,
\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}R_{i,t}
is a function of
\boldsymbol{Z}_{t}
. Then,
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}R_{i,t}\}_{t}
is a zero-mean sequence with strong mixing coefficient upper bounded by the strong mixing coefficient of
\{\boldsymbol{Z}_{t}\}_{t}
.

For the polynomial case, applying the union bound followed by Markov’s inequality we conclude that

\max_{i}\left\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\right\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}n^{1/p}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\max_{i,t,j}\left|X^{(j)}_{i,t}\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(nkT)^{1/p}.

Also, by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X_{i,t}^{(j)}R_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(p+\epsilon)/2}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X_{i,t}^{(j)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|R_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}\leq(1+C\sqrt{r})C^{2}.

Then, by Lemma
S.4
,
\max_{i,j}|\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{i}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\frac{\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}\sqrt{r}(nk)^{2/p}}{\sqrt{T}}
. Plugging the last three probability bounds back on (
S.2
) yields the result for the polynomial case.

Similarly, for the exponential case, the union bound followed by Lemma
S.20
gives us

\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}^{-1}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\log n)^{1/\gamma_{2}}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\max_{i,t,j}|X^{(j)}_{i,t}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}[\log(nkT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}.

Also, by Lemma
S.21
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X_{i,t}^{(j)}R_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X_{i,t}^{(j)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\lor{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|R_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\lesssim 1.

Hence,
\max_{i,j}|\widehat{v}^{(j)}_{i}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\sqrt{\frac{\log(nk)}{T}}
by Lemma
S.5
. Plugging the last three probability bounds back on (
S.2
) yields the result for the exponential case,

S.5.2
Proof of Theorems
2
and
S.8

The proof is an adaption of the proof of Theorem 4 and Corollary 1 in
Fan, Liao and Mincheva 2013
, henceforth FLM, to accommodate (i) the serial dependency (strong mixing sequences), (ii) polynomial tails, and (iii) the estimation error in the sample covariance matrix. For part (a), we use expression (A.1) in
Bai 2003
to obtain the following identity

\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}=\left(\frac{\boldsymbol{V}}{n}\right)^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{T}\sum_{s=1}^{T}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{s}\frac{\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{U}_{s}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{U}_{t})}{n}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{s=1}^{T}\left(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{s}\widetilde{\zeta}_{st}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{s}\widetilde{\eta}_{st}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{s}\widetilde{\xi}_{st}\right)\right],

(S.3)

where
\boldsymbol{V}
is a
(r\times r)
diagonal matrix whose diagonal is given by the eigenvalues of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}
in decreasing fashion with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}/T
; and
\widetilde{\zeta}_{st},\widetilde{\eta}_{st}
and
\widetilde{\xi}_{st}
are defined before Lemma
S.8
.

By Assumptions
3
(d) and
2
we have
\|\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}\leq r\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\|_{\max}\|\boldsymbol{F}\|_{\max}+\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}g(nT)
. Applying Lemma
S.12
we conclude that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{R}[g(nT)+\varrho_{R}]\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1
. Finally,
\psi g_{\alpha}(n)/\sqrt{T}\lesssim 1
also by assumption. Then,
\|(\frac{\boldsymbol{V}}{n})^{-1}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1
by Lemma
S.11
. Using the results (a)-(d) of Lemma
S.10
we can bound in probability each of the terms in brackets of (
S.3
) in
\ell_{2}
norm, uniformly in
t\leq T
. Result (a) follows since

\max_{t\in[T]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{g(T)}{\sqrt{n}}+g(nT)\varrho_{R}\right],

where
g(x)=x^{1/p}
under Assumption (
2
.c) and
g(x)=[\log(x)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}
under Assumption (
2
.d).

For part (b) we use the fact that
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}/T
and set
\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}=\boldsymbol{I}_{r}
to write

\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}=\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\widetilde{U}_{i,t}+\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widehat{R}_{i,t}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t})+\boldsymbol{H}\left(\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right)\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}.

(S.4)

The first term can be upper bounded in
\ell_{2}
norm, uniformly in
i\leq n
, by

\sqrt{r}\|\boldsymbol{H}\|\max_{i\leq n}\max_{j\leq r}\left|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}F_{jt}\widetilde{U}_{i,t}\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}g_{1}(n)/\sqrt{T}+\varrho_{R},

where the equality follows from Lemma
S.11
(b) and (e), and
g_{1}(x)=\mathscr{R}_{\alpha}x^{2/p}
under Assumption (
2
.c) and
g_{1}(x)=\sqrt{\log(x)}
under Assumption (
2
.d). The
\ell_{2}
-norm of the second term is upper bounded uniformly in
i\leq n
by

\left(\max_{i\leq n}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\widehat{R}_{i,t}^{2}\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|^{2}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\left[\frac{1}{T}+(1/\sqrt{n}+)^{2}\right]^{1/2},

where the first term after the equality follows from Lemma
S.11
(d) together with the theorem’s assumption and the second term from Lemma
S.9
(e). Finally, the last term of (
S.4
) is upper bounded by

\|\boldsymbol{H}\|\|\max_{i\leq n}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\left\|\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\right\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1/\sqrt{T},

where the last term is
\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1/\sqrt{T}
by the maximum inequality and Assumption
2
.
Plugging the last three displays back into (
S.4
) yields result (b).

For part (c) we have
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}=\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}-\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\prime}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Lambda}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}^{\prime}-\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{F}^{\prime}\|_{\max}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{R}}-\boldsymbol{R}\|_{\max}
. The last term is
\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{R}
by assumption. For the first term we use the decomposition

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})^{\prime}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t})+(\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})^{\prime}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t})

\displaystyle\qquad+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}+\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{H}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r})\boldsymbol{F}_{t}.

(S.5)

Therefore, we can upper bound the left hand side as

\displaystyle|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|+\|\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{F}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\|\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|+\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\|\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|\|\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{H}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\|.

Now, we bound in probability, uniformly in
i\leq n
and
t\leq T
, each of the four terms above. The first one is given by parts (a) and (b).
\max_{i\leq n}\|\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{H}\|\max_{i\leq n}||\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}r\|\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1
by Lemma
S.11
(b) and Assumption
3
(d). Thus, the second term is bounded by part (a). For the third term,
\max_{t\leq T}\|\boldsymbol{H}\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|\leq\|\boldsymbol{H}\|\max_{t\leq T}||\boldsymbol{F}_{t}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}g(T)
by Lemma
S.11
(b) and Assumption
3
. Finally,
\|\boldsymbol{H}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{H}-\boldsymbol{I}_{r}\|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}1/\sqrt{T}+1/\sqrt{n}+\varrho_{R}
by Lemma
S.11
(c). Hence, the last term is
\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}g(T)(1/\sqrt{T}+1/\sqrt{n}+\varrho_{R})
by Assumptions
3
(d) and
2
.

S.5.3
Proof of Theorems
3
and
S.9

For
i\in[n]
, let
\mathcal{Q}_{i}(\boldsymbol{a}):=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{a}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}\|_{2}^{2}/T
for
\boldsymbol{a}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We have that
L(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i})+\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\|_{1}\leq\mathcal{Q}(\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}
by definition of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}
. Also, since
\mathcal{Q}_{i}(\boldsymbol{\theta})
is a quadratic function, it implies that
(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})\leq 2\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}-\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\|_{1})
where
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}^{\prime}/T
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}:=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}/T
. By Holder’s inequality, we have
|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}^{\prime}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})|\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}
. Then, for
\xi\geq 4\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}
we have

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})\leq\xi/2\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}+\xi(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}-\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}\|_{1}).

(S.6)

For any index set
\mathcal{S}\subseteq[n]
, by the decomposability of the
\ell_{1}
norm (refer to Definition 1 in
Negahban et al. 2012
) followed by the triangle inequality, we have
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}}\|_{1}=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}\geq\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}-\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}
and
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}=\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}
. Plugging it back in (
S.6
) yields

2(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}\leq 3\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}+4\xi\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}.

(S.7)

We then conclude that any minimizer of (
3.5
) obeys
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathcal{S}_{i},3):=\{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}^{c}_{i}}\|_{1}\leq 3\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\|_{1}\|_{1}\}
where
\mathcal{S}_{i}:=\{j:\theta_{i,j}\neq 0\}
is the support of
\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}
.

Recall the definition of the compatibility constant appearing in
van de Geer and
Bühlmann 2009
, reproduced below for convenience.

Definition 1
.

For an
n\times n
matrix
\boldsymbol{M}
, a set
\mathcal{S}\subseteq\{1,\ldots,n\}
and a scalar
\zeta\geq 0
, the compatibility constant is given by

\kappa(\boldsymbol{M},\mathcal{S},\zeta):=\inf\left\{\frac{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\boldsymbol{M}}\sqrt{|\mathcal{S}|}}{\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}}:\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}:\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}^{c}}\|_{1}\leq\xi\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}\right\},

(S.8)

where
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{\boldsymbol{M}}=\sqrt{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}{\boldsymbol{M}}\boldsymbol{x}}
.
Moreover, we say that
(\boldsymbol{M},\mathcal{S},\zeta)
satisfies the compatibility condition if
\kappa(\boldsymbol{M},\mathcal{S},\zeta)>0
.

Notice that the square of the compatibility constant is close related to the minimum of the
\ell_{1}
-norm of the eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{M}
, restricted to a cone in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. By definition of the compatibility constant
\widehat{\kappa}_{i}:=\kappa(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i},\mathcal{S}_{0},3)
, we have that
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i,\mathcal{S}}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i,\mathcal{S}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})}\sqrt{|\mathcal{S}_{i}|}/\widehat{\kappa}_{i}
. Apply this inequality to (
S.7
) and use the fact that
4ab<a^{2}+4b^{2}
for non-negative
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, to obtain

(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\leq 4\xi^{2}|\mathcal{S}_{i}|/\widehat{\kappa}_{i}^{2},

(S.9)

provided that
\xi\geq 4\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}:=\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}^{\prime})
and
\kappa_{i}:=\kappa(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i},\mathcal{S}_{0},3)
. Note that
\min_{i}\kappa_{i}^{2}\geq b^{2}>0
under Assumption (
2
.g) because

\lambda_{\min}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}_{t}\boldsymbol{\mathcal{U}}_{t}^{\prime}\right)\right]\leq\min_{i}\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i})\leq\min_{i}\min_{\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathcal{S}_{i},3)}\left\{\frac{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}}{\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}}\right\}\leq\min_{i}\kappa_{i}^{2},

where in the first inequality we use Cauchy interlacing theorem and in the last on we use the fact that
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}\geq\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}
and the bound
\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\|_{1}\leq\sqrt{|\mathcal{S}_{i}}\|\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathcal{S}_{i}}\|_{2}
.
Also, we may use Lemma
S.12
with
\zeta=3
and
\alpha=1/2
to assert that if
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}\leq\xi_{1}
for some
\xi_{1}>0
, such that
32\xi_{1}|\mathcal{S}_{i}|/\kappa_{i}^{2}\leq 1
then,
\widehat{\kappa}_{i}^{2}\geq\kappa_{i}^{2}/2
and
\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}/2\leq\boldsymbol{x}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}\boldsymbol{x}
for
\boldsymbol{x}\in\mathbb{C}(\mathcal{S}_{i},3)
. Use the last three inequalities in (
S.9
) to conclude that, for
i\in[n]:

\tfrac{1}{2}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})+\xi\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\leq 8\xi^{2}|\mathcal{S}_{i}|/b^{2},

(S.10)

provided that
\xi\geq 4\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}
and
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}\leq\xi_{1}
such that
32\xi_{1}|\mathcal{S}_{i}|/b\leq 1
.

S.5.3.1
Probabilistic Bounds

We now bound in probability the events
\{\xi\geq 4\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}\}
and
\{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}\leq\xi_{1}\}
, uniformly in
i\in[n]
. For the former, by definition of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}
, we have
\max_{i,t}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}
and
\max_{i,t}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}
. Also, by the definition of
V_{i,t}
, the triangle inequality followed by Hölder’s inequality, we have
\max_{i,t}|V_{i,t}|\leq\max_{i,t}|U_{i,t}|+\max_{i,t}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\boldsymbol{|}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\leq(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}
. Similarly, we conclude
\max_{i,t}|\widehat{V}_{i,t}-V_{i,t}|\leq(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}
.

First, we decompose

T\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}=\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{V}_{i,\cdot}+\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{V}_{i,\cdot})+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot})\boldsymbol{V}_{i,\cdot}+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot})(\widehat{\boldsymbol{V}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{V}_{i,\cdot}),

and bound each term individually as

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}(\widehat{V}_{i,\cdot}-V_{i,\cdot})/T\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,t}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\max_{i,t}|\widehat{V}_{i,t}-V_{i,t}|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max},

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot})V_{i,\cdot}/T\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,t}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\max_{i,t}|V_{i,t}|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max},

and

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,\cdot}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot})(\widehat{V}_{i,\cdot}-V_{i,\cdot})/T\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i,t}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\max_{i,t}|\widehat{V}_{i,t}-V_{i,t}|

\displaystyle\leq(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}^{2}.

Then,

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{V}_{i,\cdot}/T\|_{\infty}

(S.11)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})(2\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}^{2}).

We now bound
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}
and
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}
. For the former,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}\leq\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}
and for the latter

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}

\displaystyle={\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}+{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{\psi}.

(S.12)

By definition, we have that
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}V_{i,t}=f_{i}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t},\dots,\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t-l})
for some mapping
f_{i}
, for each
i\in[n]
and
t\in[T]
. Then, by Lemma
S.1
, we have that the strong mixing coefficients of the sequence
\{W_{i,t}^{(j)}V_{i,t}\}_{t}
are such that
\widetilde{\alpha}_{m}\leq\alpha_{(m-l)\lor 0}
, where
W_{i,t}^{(j)}
denote the
j
-th component of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}
for
j\in[d]
.

Under Assumption
2
(c), by the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality and the last two bounds, we have

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j)}V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p/2+\epsilon/2}

\displaystyle\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq(1+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}^{2}\leq C^{2}\left(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2}\right).

Therefore, by Lemma
S.4
followed by Lemma
S.1
, we conclude that for
p\in[4,\infty)

\max_{i,j}|\sum_{t=1}^{T}W_{i,t}^{(j)}V_{i,t}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\left(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2}\right)\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\left[n(l+1)\right]^{2/p}\sqrt{T},

where
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}:=\big(\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}(m+1)^{(p/2)-2}\alpha_{(m-l)\lor 0}^{1-p/(p+\epsilon)}\big)^{2/p}
. By assumption
\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{U}
, and the union bound followed by Markov’s inequality give us
\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}nT^{1/p}
. Then, from (
S.11
), we obtain

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\big[n(l+1)\big]^{2/p}T^{-1/2}

(S.13)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\left[(nT)^{1/p}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}\right]=:\xi_{0}.

Similarly, under Assumption
2
(d) and Lemma
S.21
we have

\displaystyle{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j)}V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j)}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\lor{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\lesssim(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2}).

Therefore, by Lemma
S.5
we conclude that

\max_{i,j}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}W_{i,t}^{(j)}V_{i,t}\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\sqrt{T\log[n(l+1)]}.

The union bound followed by Lemma
S.20
give us
\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}
. Finally, we use (
S.11
) once again to obtain

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{E}}_{i}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{2})\sqrt{\log[n(l+1)]}T^{-1/2}

(S.14)

\displaystyle\qquad+(1+\max_{i}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\left\{[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}\right\}:=\xi_{0}.

We now bound
\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}
. Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}:=\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,\cdot}^{\prime}/T
. From the definition of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}
and Lemma
S.12
we have that

\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}\|_{\max}\leq\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}(2\|\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{U}}-\boldsymbol{U}\|_{\max}).

Also by definition, we have that
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}^{\prime}=g_{i}(\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t},\dots,\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t-l})
for some function
g_{i}
for each
i\in[n]
and
t\in[T]
. Then, by Lemma
S.1
we have that the strong mixing coefficients of the sequence
\{\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}^{\prime}\}_{t}
,
\check{\alpha}_{m}\leq\alpha_{(m-l)\lor 0}
. Let
W_{i,t}^{(j)}
denote the
j
-th component of
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}
for
j\in[d]
.

Under Assumption
2
(c) and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality we have

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{1})}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p/2+\epsilon/2}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{1})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}\leq\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}^{2}\leq C^{2};\qquad j_{1},j_{2}\in[d].

Therefore, by Lemma
S.4
followed by Lemma
S.1
, we conclude that for
p\in[4,\infty)

\max_{i,j_{1},j_{2}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}W_{i,t}^{(j)}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}-\mathbb{E}\left(W_{i,t}^{(j)}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right)\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\left[n(l+1)\right]^{4/p}\sqrt{T},

where
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}
is defined above. Hence, by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}+\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}\|_{\max}

(S.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\frac{\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\big[n(l+1)\big]^{4/p}}{\sqrt{T}}+(nT)^{1/p}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}=:\xi_{1}.

Similarly, under Assumption
2
(d) and Lemma
S.21
we have, for
j_{1},j_{2}\in[d]
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{1})}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{1})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\lor{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}\leq C_{\gamma_{2}}\sup_{t}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\boldsymbol{U}_{\cdot,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\leq C_{\gamma_{2}}C.

Thus, by Lemma
S.5
we conclude that

\max_{i,j_{1},j_{2}}\left|\sum_{t=1}^{T}W_{i,t}^{(j)}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}-\mathbb{E}\left(W_{i,t}^{(j)}W_{i,t}^{(j_{2})}\right)\right|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\sqrt{T\log[n(l+1)]}.

Therefore, by the triangle inequality

\displaystyle\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{i}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{i}\|_{\max}+\max_{i}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i}\|_{\max}

(S.16)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\sqrt{\frac{\log[n(l+1)]}{T}}+[\log(nT)]^{1/\gamma_{2}}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{U}^{2}=:\xi_{1}.

S.5.4
Proof of Theorems
4
and
S.10

Decompose the prediction error as

\displaystyle\widehat{Y}_{i,t}-\widetilde{Y}_{i,t}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{G}_{t}+\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}+\big[\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P})\big](\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{G}_{t})-(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P})\boldsymbol{G}_{t}

\displaystyle\qquad+\big[\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})\big]^{\prime}(\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t})+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t},

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}

\displaystyle=\big[\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\big](\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{P})+(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i})\boldsymbol{P}.

For the first term we have

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}\left|(\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i})^{\prime}\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\right|\leq\max_{i\in[n]}\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{i}\|_{1}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{\gamma}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}).

For the second term,

\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\varrho_{P}+\varrho_{\lambda})

and, therefore,

\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{P}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{G}}_{t}-\boldsymbol{\lambda}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{P}\boldsymbol{G}_{t}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\varrho_{P}+\varrho_{\lambda}+\varrho_{F}).

Finally, for the last term, Hölder’s inequality yields

\displaystyle|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}|

\displaystyle\leq(\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1})\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}+\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}.

As a consequence,

\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{i}^{\prime}\widehat{\boldsymbol{W}}_{i,t}-\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}^{\prime}\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}|\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}\varrho_{U}+\varrho_{\theta}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}

and

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}|\widehat{Y}_{i,t}-\widetilde{Y}_{i,t}|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}\varrho_{\gamma}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}+\varrho_{U}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{\theta}_{i}\|_{1}+\varrho_{\theta}\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}+\varrho_{P}+\varrho_{\lambda}+\varrho_{F}.

Finally,
\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}n^{1/p}
in the polynomial case and
\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{X}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\log n)^{1/\gamma_{2}}
in the exponential one. Similarly,
\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}d_{W}^{1/p}
in the polynomial case and
\max_{i\in[n]}\|\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}\|_{\infty}\lesssim_{\mathbb{P}}(\log d_{W})^{1/\gamma_{2}}
in the exponential one.

S.5.5
Proof of Theorems
5
and
S.11

For
\mathcal{D}\in[n]^{2}
, let
\boldsymbol{N}_{t}:=(N_{1,t},\dots,N_{d,t})^{\prime}:=\left[U_{i,t}U_{j,t}-\mathbb{E}\left(U_{i,t}U_{j,t}\right):(i,j)\in\mathcal{D}\right]
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{N}}_{t}:=\left[\widehat{U}_{i,t}\widehat{U}_{j,t}-\mathbb{E}\left(U_{i,t}U_{j,t}\right):(i,j)\in\mathcal{D}\right]
where
d:=|\mathcal{D}|
. Then,

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t\in[T]}\boldsymbol{N}_{t}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t\in[T]}\widehat{\boldsymbol{N}}_{t}.

For the polynomial case (Theorems
5
), by Cauchy Schwartz inequality and Assumption
2
,

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}U_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(p+\epsilon)/2}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}\leq C^{2}.

Then,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|N_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(p+\epsilon)/2}\leq 2C^{2}
for
j\in[d]
; for the exponential case (Theorem
S.11
). By Lemma
S.21
and Assumption
4
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|U_{i,t}U_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}\lesssim 1
. Then,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|N_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}\lesssim 1
for
j\in[d]
. Also, the mixing coefficient of
\{\boldsymbol{N}_{t},t\in[T]\}
are upper bounded by those of
\{\boldsymbol{U}_{t},t\in[T]\}
by Lemma
S.1
.
Therefore, we can apply Theorem
S.13
(a) to bound
\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})
with “
p=p/2
” and “
\epsilon=\epsilon/2
” for the polynomial case and the first result of Theorem
5
follows. Similarly, we can apply Theorem
S.13
(b) to bound
\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})
with “
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}
” and “
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{2}/2
” for the exponential case and the first result of Theorem
S.11
follows.

By triangle inequality followed Lemma
S.14
(applied twice) and Corollary 1 in
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Kato 2013b
we have

\displaystyle\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\leq\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}})+\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})+\inf_{\delta_{1}>0}\left\{\Delta(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}},\delta_{1})+\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}\|_{\infty}>\delta_{1})\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}})+\inf_{\delta_{1}>0}\left\{\delta_{1}\sqrt{1\lor\log d}+\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}\|_{\infty}>\delta_{1})\right\},

where
\Delta(\cdot,\cdot)
is defined by (
S.1
). Recall that
\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}
is the variance of
\boldsymbol{N}_{t}
and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*}|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}\sim N(\boldsymbol{0},\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}})
. Then, on the event
\{\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}\|_{\max}\leq\delta_{2}\}
for
\delta_{2}>0
by Theorem 1.1 in
Fang and Koike 2021
(see also Lemma 2.1 in
Chernozhukov, Chetverikov and
Koike 2020
for this particular application)

\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}\|_{\infty}\leq t)-\mathbb{P}(\|\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*}\|_{\infty}\leq t|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})|\lesssim\delta_{2}\log d(1\lor|\log d|),

and

\rho(\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*})\lesssim\inf_{\delta_{2}>0}\left\{\delta_{2}\log d(1\lor|\log d|)+\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}\|_{\max}>\delta_{2})\right\}.

S.5.6
Proof of Corollary
3

Since
S^{*}|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y}
has no point-mass, we have that
\tau=\mathbb{P}(S^{*}\leq c^{*}(\tau)|\boldsymbol{X},\boldsymbol{Y})
almost surely for every
\tau\in(0,1)
and
\mathcal{D}\in[n]^{2}
. Then, under the Null, using the notation as in the proof of Theorem
5
, we write for
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
:

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathcal{D}}\sup_{\tau}\left|\mathbb{P}\left[S\leq c^{*}(\tau)\right]-\tau\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{\mathcal{D}}\sup_{\tau}\left|\mathbb{P}\left[S\leq c^{*}(\tau)\right]-\mathbb{P}(S^{*}\leq c^{*}(\tau)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathcal{D}}\rho(\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}},\boldsymbol{Z}^{*})

\displaystyle\lesssim\varrho_{\Sigma}+\delta_{1}\sqrt{1\lor\log n}+\sup_{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}-\widetilde{\boldsymbol{J}}\|_{\infty}>\delta_{1})

\displaystyle\quad+\delta_{2}\log n(1\lor|\log n|)+\sup_{\mathcal{D}}\mathbb{P}(\|\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}-\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}\|_{\max}>\delta_{2}).

Let
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
denote the rate appearing on Lemmas
S.15
and
S.16
, respectively, with
\varrho_{U}
equals to the rate of Collorary
1
and
\mathcal{D}=[n]^{2}
. By assumption,
\varrho_{\Sigma}\lor(\log n)^{3/2}\gamma_{1}\lor(\log n)^{3}\gamma_{2}=o(1)
as
T,n\to\infty
when we set
\delta_{1}=\gamma_{1}\log n
and
\delta_{2}=\gamma_{2}\log n
. Therefore, all the terms in the last expression vanish in probability, and the result follows.

S.5.7
Proof of Theorems
6
and
S.12

The proof is parallel to the proof of Theorem
5
. We outline the main differences. For
\mathcal{D}\in[n]^{2}
, let
\boldsymbol{K}_{t}:=(K_{1,t},\dots,K_{d,t})^{\prime}:=\left[V_{i,j,t}V_{j,i,t}-\mathbb{E}(V_{i,j,t}V_{j,i,t}):(i,j)\in\mathcal{D}\right]
and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{K}}_{t}:=\left[\widehat{V}_{i,j,t}\widehat{V}_{j,i,t}-\mathbb{E}(V_{i,j,t}V_{j,i,t}):(i,j)\in\mathcal{D}\right]
where
d:=|\mathcal{D}|
. Then,

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t\in[T]}\boldsymbol{K}_{t}\quad\textnormal{and}\quad\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t\in[T]}\widehat{\boldsymbol{K}}_{t}.

For the polynomial case (Theorem
6
), by Cauchy Schwartz inequality, the bound in (
S.12
) with
\boldsymbol{W}_{i,t}=\boldsymbol{U}_{-ij,t}
, and Assumption
2
, we write

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,j,t}V_{j,i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(p+\epsilon)/2}\leq{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{j,i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}\leq C^{2}_{\chi},

where
C_{\chi}:=(1+\max_{i,j\in\mathcal{D}}\|\boldsymbol{\chi}_{i,j}\|_{2})C
. Then,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|K_{j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{(p+\epsilon)/2}\leq 2C^{2}_{\chi}
for
j\in[d]
. Also, the mixing coefficient of
\{\boldsymbol{K}_{t},t\in[T]\}
are upper bounded by those of
\{\boldsymbol{U}_{t},t\in[T]\}
by Lemma
S.1
.
Therefore, we can apply Theorem
S.13
(a) to bound
\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})
with “
p=p/2
” and “
\epsilon=\epsilon/2
” and the first result of Theorem
6
follows. The other two results can be obtained as in the proof do Theorem
5
, with
\widehat{\boldsymbol{J}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}}^{*}
replaced by
\widehat{\boldsymbol{Q}}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}
, and
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}}^{*}
, respectively.

For the exponential case (Theorem
S.12
), we replace the Cauchy Schwartz inequality and Assumption
2
used above by Lemma
S.21
and Assumption
4
, respectively, and conclude that

{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,j,t}V_{j,i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}/2}}\lesssim{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{i,j,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\lor{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|V_{j,i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{e^{\gamma_{2}}}^{2}\leq C^{2}_{\chi}.

Finally, we apply Theorem
S.13
(b) to bound
\rho(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{Q}},\boldsymbol{\mathcal{H}})
with “
\gamma_{1}=\gamma_{1}
” and “
\gamma_{2}=\gamma_{2}/2
” and the first of Theorem
S.12
follows.

S.5.8
Proof of Corollary
4

The proof is identical to the proof of Corollary
3
with Lemmas
S.15
and
S.16
replaced by Lemmas
S.17
and
S.18
, respectively.

S.6
High-Dimension Central Limit Theorem for Strong Mixing sequences

The setup for this section is the following. Let
\{\boldsymbol{X}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{X}_{T}\}
be a sequence of zero-mean random vectors taking value in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, whose strong mixing coefficient we denote by
\{\alpha_{n}:n\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Define
\boldsymbol{S}=T^{-1/2}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}
and let
\boldsymbol{Z}\sim N(0,\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
where
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}(\boldsymbol{S}\boldsymbol{S}^{\prime})
. The next result upper bounds

\rho(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{Z}):=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{R}}|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{S}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{Z}\in A)|,

where
\mathcal{R}
is the class of all rectangles in the from
\bigtimes_{j=1}^{d}(a_{j},b_{j}]
for some
-\infty\leq a_{j}\leq b_{j}\leq\infty
and
j\in[d]
. We also define for
s>0

\Delta(\boldsymbol{Z},s):=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\{\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{Z}\leq z+s)-\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{Z}\leq z)\}.

(S.1)

Note that we may assume, without loss of generality, that all the elements on the diagonal of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are positive. Otherwise, we could exclude the entries of
\boldsymbol{S}
(and
\boldsymbol{Z})
that are
0
almost surely. Also, since
\rho(\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{D}\boldsymbol{Z})=\rho(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{Z})
for any diagonal matrix
\boldsymbol{D}
, we may assume that all the elements in the diagonal of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
are
1
, i.e.,
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a correlation matrix. Finally, we assume that
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is positive definite and denote by
\sigma_{*}^{2}\in(0,1)
its smallest eigenvalue.

Theorem S.13
.

[High-Dimension Central Limit Theorem for Strong Mixing sequences]

1.

Polynomial Case
: If for some
p\in[4,\infty)
and
\epsilon>0
,
{\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|\kern-1.07639pt\left|X_{i,t}\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|\kern-1.07639pt\right|}_{p+\epsilon}\leq B
for
i\in[d]
and
t\in[T]
for some constant
B
that might dependent on
T
and
p
; and
\alpha_{n}\leq Kn^{-r}
for
r\geq 0
and a constant
K
that might depend on
d
, then

\displaystyle\rho\left(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{B^{2}}{\sigma_{*}^{2}}\left[\frac{\mathscr{A}_{2}}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\mathscr{A}_{1}}{T^{(1/2)-\kappa}}+\frac{K^{1-2/p}}{T^{(r/2)(1-2/p)-1}}\right]\log T\log d

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(B\mathscr{A}_{\sqrt{T},4})^{2}(\log d)^{3/2}\log T}{T^{1/4}\sigma_{*}^{2}}+\frac{(B\mathscr{A}_{\sqrt{T},p})^{2}d^{2/p}(\log d)^{2}\log T}{T^{1/2-1/p}\sigma_{*}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{\left[B\mathscr{A}_{\sqrt{T},p}d(\log d)^{(3/2)p-4}\log T\log(dT)\right]}{T^{1/4}\sigma_{*}^{p/(p-2)}}^{1/(p-2)},

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{1}{T^{r\kappa-1/2}}\left[d^{1/(p+1)}(\mathscr{A}_{T^{\kappa},p}B)^{D_{p}}\sqrt{1\lor\log d}+K\right],

where
\kappa:=\frac{1/2+D_{p}/4}{r+D_{p}/2}\land 1/2
,
D_{p}:=\frac{p}{p+1}
,
\mathscr{A}_{k,p}:=\left[\sum_{n=0}^{k-1}(n+1)^{p/2-2}\alpha_{n}^{\epsilon/(p+\epsilon)}\right]^{\tfrac{2}{p}}
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathscr{A}_{1}:=\sum_{n=0}^{\lfloor T^{\kappa}\rfloor}\alpha_{n}^{1-2/p}
, and
\mathscr{A}_{2}:=\sum_{n=1}^{\lfloor\sqrt{T}\rfloor}n\alpha_{n}^{1-2/p}
. In particular, if
r>(\tfrac{p+\epsilon}{\epsilon})(p/2-1)\lor\tfrac{2}{1-2/p}
then
\mathscr{A}_{k,p},\mathscr{A}_{1}
and
\mathscr{A}_{2}
can be bounded by constant that does not depend on
T
or
d
.

2.

Exponential Case
: If
\{X_{i,t}:t\in[T]\}
fulfills the conditions of Lemma
S.3
for
i\in[d]
, then for
T\geq 16\lor(C\log d)^{1/\gamma-1/2}
,
d\geq 2
, and
d\sqrt{T}\geq\exp(1/\gamma)-1
,

\displaystyle\rho\left(\boldsymbol{S}_{X},\boldsymbol{Z}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log d+\big[\log(dT)\big]^{2/\gamma}(\log d)^{2}\log T}{\sqrt{T}\sigma_{*}^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{(\log d)^{2}+(\log d)^{3/2}\log T+\log d(\log T)^{\gamma_{1}+1}\log(dT)}{T^{1/4}\sigma_{*}^{2}}.

We adapt the classical “big block-small block” technique commonly used to prove central limit theorems with dependence. Recall we assume
T\geq 4
and consider two sequences of positive integers
a:=a_{T}
and
b:=b_{T}
such that
b\leq a
and
a+b\leq T/2
. Let
m:=\lceil T/(a+b)\rceil
and define for
j\in[m-1]
consecutive blocks of size
a
and
b

with index set
\mathcal{A}_{j}:=\{((j-1)(a+b)+1,\dots,(j-1)(a+b)+a\}
and
\mathcal{B}_{j}:=\{(j-1)(a+b)+a+1,\dots j(a+b)\}
.
Finally, set
\mathcal{A}_{m}:=\{m(a+b)+1,\dots,T\}
, which might be empty. Define,

\boldsymbol{A}_{j}:=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{A}_{j}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t},\quad j\in[m]\qquad\textnormal{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{B}_{j}=\sum_{t\in\mathcal{B}_{j}}\boldsymbol{X}_{t},\qquad j\in[m-1]

such that

\boldsymbol{S}:=\boldsymbol{S}_{X}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\boldsymbol{X}_{t}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\boldsymbol{A}_{j}+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\boldsymbol{B}_{j}=:\boldsymbol{S}_{A}+\boldsymbol{S}_{B}.

Also, let
\{\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}:j\in[m]\}
be an independent sequence such that
\boldsymbol{A}_{j}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}
have the same distribution for
j\in[m]
, and define
\boldsymbol{S}_{\widetilde{A}}:=T^{-1/2}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{A}}_{j}
.

We start by applying Lemma
S.14
with
X=\boldsymbol{S}_{X}
,
Y=\boldsymbol{S}_{A}
and
d(x,y)=\|x-y\|_{\infty}
to obtain, for
A\in\mathcal{R}
and
s>0
,

\displaystyle|\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{S}_{X}\in A)-\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{S}_{A}\in A)|\leq\mathbb{P}(\|\boldsymbol{S}_{B}\|_{\infty}>s)+\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{S}_{A}\in A^{s}\setminus A)\lor\mathbb{P}(\boldsymbol{S}_{A}\in A\setminus A^{-s}).

The right hand side of the last expression can be upper bounded as
